But I love shounen manga so I the mc to be 18 but dress & and be as short as a 10 year old.

7. Tell me everything you have so far, even messy fragments. => Main Character (after time travel) 18 years old but is cursed to look like he’s 10 after going back in time. He has blond hair. Hair style is like Kazuto kitigiyas from sword art online. And wears the clothing style of the kid from idolize 7 who wear the clothes of a little boy as the “young style” idol group character. He is as short as a 10 year old. Wears a blue shirt with red sleeves (on the volume cover)

Main character (before the time travel) black hair. Black highschool Japanese uniform clothes and looks 18.

Opening of the volume. MC is walking on a cherry blossom tree filled road when he is transported back in time to Egypt during the reign of Tutankhamen. He is then cursed to look like a 10 year old until he dies by the god he is transported by because he says “ I wish I was a kid again, because life was simple” while complaining about his life right before he is transported back in time.

After transport: he is captured by gayards after stealing food from a stall but actually he tries to pay with a 1000 yen bill. Then he is somehow brought in front of the pharaoh who is still a child as well. He is brought to be his playmate slave.

That’s all I got for the story so far.

Act 2: Power & Betrayal—He gains influence but a court conspiracy threatens his mission. Act 3: Revelation & Sacrifice—He exposes the conspiracy, reshapes Egypt's fate, but faces the cost of breaking his curse.

421. Real historical events that inspire you => Tutankhamun's brief reign amid court intrigue: a young ruler manipulated by powerful advisors like Ay and Horemheb, facing pressure to maintain Egypt's stability during political chaos.; The fall of the Hittite Empire: internal betrayals and succession conflicts that weakened a once-mighty civilization, showing how individual corruption cascades into societal collapse.; Ancient Egypt's rigid class system: how commoners and slaves navigated oppressive hierarchies by strategic positioning, hidden identities, and calculated loyalties to survive court politics.; The Amarna Period's upheaval: radical religious and social reforms that destabilized Egypt, forcing ordinary people to choose between personal survival and loyalty to collapsing traditions.
422. Myths, legends or folklore that inspire you => The curse of the child-king: Ancient Egyptian folklore about gods trapping souls in youth as punishment, forcing them to witness empires crumble while powerless.; The oracle's bargain: A mythic tradition where time-travelers must sacrifice their original form to prevent apocalypse, becoming living ghosts in foreign eras.; Serpent's shadow legend: Folklore of hidden advisors who whisper truth through childish mouths, worshipped as divine vessels by those who recognize them.; The two-year flame: Ancient myth of souls given exactly 24 moons to reshape destiny before returning to death, their wisdom the only weapon against fate.

432. Describe the world in a paragraph, as if writing the opening narration. => Kemet endures under a child-pharaoh's rule, its throne surrounded by vultures in human form. Ancient power struggles mask a deeper truth: destiny itself has sent a curse-bound stranger to reshape Egypt's fate.
The black land of Kemet bleeds with ambition. Priests whisper of omens, generals scheme for power, and a mysterious boy—cursed to wear a child's face—arrives as fate's instrument in a kingdom teetering toward ruin.
Kemet's golden age dims beneath shadow and intrigue. A young pharaoh sits imprisoned by courtiers' machinations while an impossible outsider, trapped in a child's body, becomes the only force capable of preventing civilizational collapse.
In Kemet, the Nile flows eternal, but its people drown in deception. A curse has brought an ancient soul to a dying kingdom, where survival demands becoming the very monster one fears most.

444. What do outsiders think of this world or country? What do locals think? => Outsiders view Kemet as a fading empire clinging to old gods. Locals see themselves as eternal—Egypt's weakness is temporary, its destiny unshakeable.

Merchants and neighbors perceive Kemet's internal chaos as opportunity for trade dominance. Citizens know their true enemy isn't external—it's the shadow council poisoning their pharaoh.
Rival kingdoms smell blood, viewing Egypt as vulnerable. Ordinary Egyptians cling to pride in Kemet's ancient legacy, refusing to admit the throne's corruption runs deep.

703. Sum up the world's history in a few sentences. => Kemet rose from the Nile's gifts, but successive pharaohs weakened the throne. Now child-king Tutankhamen inherits a fractured empire where priests and generals vie for control, and whispers speak of divine intervention.
Once, Kemet's pharaohs commanded gods themselves. Centuries of conquest drained their power. The gods, watching Egypt crumble, curse a stranger across time to prevent the child-pharaoh's ruin and restore balance.
Kemet's glory faded as plague and famine struck. The priesthood monopolized power while generals plotted. Tutankhamen's ascension promised renewal, but court vultures circle. Only fate's messenger can save the black land.
Kemet endured invasions and internal strife for generations. The current pharaoh's youth left a power vacuum—advisors now rule in shadow. The gods send a curse-bound outsider to tip the scales toward survival or destruction.

742. Explain the power system in a few sentences, as simply as you can. => Divine Whispers: The gods speak to chosen mortals through cryptic visions and compulsions. The protagonist hears fragmented commands from an ancient deity, guiding him but slowly consuming his free will.
Chronos Imprint: Time travelers bear invisible marks that grant heightened perception—reading intentions, sensing danger, predicting movements—but fade as the person ages in the new timeline.
Pharaonic Resonance: Proximity to the pharaoh amplifies one's influence over court politics. Whispered words gain weight, alliances solidify faster. Distance weakens this supernatural charisma entirely.
Curse Paradox: The protagonist's child-form grants immunity to poison and assassination attempts meant for adults, but prevents him from wielding weapons or commanding respect through physical presence alone.

1823. List the five most important relationships in Volume 1 and how each one changes. => 1. Kael & The pharaoh: Kael is the Pharoah's playmate at first and then as volume 1 progresses he gains Tutankhamun's trust and nudges him to pursue the true power that comes with being an Egyptian Pharaoh.
2. Kael & Amenhotep: Kael & Amenhotep hate each other. Amenhotep at first dismisses Kael because of his child like appearence. However, he later realizes that he is wise beyond his years and is a threat to his path to power. So he attempts to assasinate the pharoah and Kael together with poision closer to the end of volume 1.
3. Kael & Nakhti: Nakhti is Kael's mentor. At first he sees him as merely a child but later comes to love him as a son. However, his lessons are strict and hands on. He teaches Kael how to survive in the palace. They both develop great respect for each other.
4. Kael & Kalici: Kalici at first tries to frame Kael after being commissioned by Amenhotep the Vizier to do so: she is supposed to run from his room screaming that he attacked her, with bruises the Vizier's steward gave her. However, she can't make herself scream, and Kael quietly asks who hurt her instead of calling the guards. He comforts her emotionally throughout the night as she tells him about her father's death. After this Kalici becomes fiercely loyal to Kael. At the end of Volume 1's last scene Kael realizes how much he trusts her but does not say it; he has an internal monologue about it instead.
5. The pharaoh & Aneb: These two are very close from the beginning. Being a pharaoh and a slave (Aneb) Aneb has taken care of the Pharoah since he was little and has sworn to serve him and see his dreams come true.

1835. Summarize Volume 1's story from beginning to end. A paragraph or more. => Chapter 1: Kael is walking to school under cherry blossom tress when the oldest cherry blossom tree on the road suddenly splits and falls on him, killing him. He is then transported to Ancient Egypt during the reign of Tutankhamun. He realizes his body looks like he is 10 years old now due to meeting the God of the Past after dying and him granting the request Kael made before dying. "I wish i was a kid again, things were so much more simple." He ends up standing in the middle of the capital street market when he tries to buy food with foreign currency (10,000 Yen bill). The guards capture him for being a foreigner. They bring him in front of the Pharoah and the Pharaoh must decide his fate. Because the Pharoah (Tutankhamun) is still a 9 year old child and has just taken over as ruler, he has no real power yet. Therefore he decides to make Kael his playmate to save his life. 
Chapter 2: Kael is playing with Tutankhamun when an assasination attempt is made on his life. This is a regular occurance so the pharaoh does not move. He simply waits for the Commander of the Royal Retainers and Shield of the Living Pharaoh, Nakhtmin to protect him from harm as he always does. Kael is stunned and realizes he will have to learn Ancient Egyptian Politics. He leaves the Pharoah's bed chambers and seeks to find a mentor. He finds Nakhti to be his mentor. The last part of chapter 2 is Nakhti agreeing to teach him how to survive in Egyptian Court Politics. He goes through a list of the most powerful people and those he should be wary of and those he can trust. He mentions the two different factions: The Vizier's faction (Amenhotep) & the Pharoah's faction. Chapter 2 ends with a deep thematic line from Nakhti about how wise Kael is for his age. 
Chapter 3: The chapter opens with the Introduction of Amenhotep the Vizier. He is talking to Kalici (child servant) & orders her to go to Kael's chamber that night, then run out screaming that the foreign boy attacked her so Kael will be executed. When she hesitates, he says his steward will make sure her bruises are convincing. If she obeys, she can live in the palace under his protection. The next scene is Kael laying in his bed chamber when Kalici walks in with a sad depressed look on her face, her bruised arm hidden in her sleeve. She draws a breath to scream but can't make the sound. Kael sees her bruises and quietly asks who hurt her. He tells her she doesnt have to do this and asks what makes her feel so sad. Kalici's backstory is revealed and Kael comforts her emotionally throughout the night. At dawn she slips out without ever screaming, with a fierce, quiet devotion to the boy who protected her.
Chapter 4: Chapter 4 starts with Amenhotep wanting a report from Kalici to hear how the frame job went. Kalici decides to tell Amenhotep a lie: that she lost her nerve, couldn't make herself scream and accuse him, and ran out of his room in fear. She did this to try to become a double agent for Kael, the boy she is now loyal to. She purposely left out that Kael was kind to her and that they talked through the night about the death of her father. Kalici kindly asks Amenhotep to give her a different task to stay in the palace. Amenhotep agrees but says he will give her the task later. Kalici can tell he is lying and that he either plans to kill her or exile her from the palace. Kalici runs to Kael (the protagonist) for help. Kael agrees. They rush to an audience with the pharaoh and pleede to get Kalici to become a handmaid for Kael. The pharoh agrees and makes an official announcement. This saves Kalici's life. The last scene opens up in the throne room 1 week later where Tutankhamun tells Kael (his playmate & protagonist) about how his father was really killed. He was assasinated with poision. Kael is shocked and realizes all over again that even the ruler of egypt isnt safe from danger like this. He thinks "How safe am I?

1850. Describe Volume 1 as a movie trailer: the key shots, in order. => Cherry petals falling on a school road, then the oldest tree cracking and falling. A void of ancient sundials and a voice: "Simple? We shall see." A blond ten-year-old in the Thebes market holding up a 10,000 yen bill. Rope on small wrists before a gold throne, and a nine-year-old king saying "My playmate." An assassin's blade stopped an inch from the boy king, who never puts down his game piece. A frightened servant girl with a bruised arm who cannot scream. A memorial feast with every seat the same as the night a king died, and a cup knocked to the floor. A lamp lifted to a kneeling girl's face in a dark corridor. Smoke, a falling blade and a masked stranger dropping from the ceiling. Black screen. Kael's voice: "They see a child." Title card.
1851. Describe the first scene of Volume 1. => Kael, eighteen, black-haired, in his black school uniform, walks to school alone on a cherry blossom road in spring. His phone buzzes with messages he doesn't open, and he's dreading an exam. He passes Mrs. Hayashi sweeping petals outside her tea shop, watches small kids play, and says out loud that he wishes he was ten again because life was simpler. With a crack like a gunshot, the oldest cherry tree splits at its roots and falls on him. Petals storm across the pavement, and everything goes black.

1871. Describe the first few pages as a list of beats. => 1. Cold open, one illustration page: guards drag a small blond boy with tied hands toward a gold throne (the flash-forward). 2. Cut to Japan: cherry petals, Kael walking alone, phone buzzing. 3. Mrs. Hayashi sweeping, small kids laughing. 4. Kael's wish, said out loud. 5. The oldest cherry tree cracks at its roots and falls on him: petals, weight, black. 6. A hall of ancient sundials and the God of the Past: "Simple? We shall see." 7. Blinding sunlight and the smell of fish and myrrh: the Thebes market.
1872. What event kicks off the story? => Kael is killed when the oldest cherry tree on his walk to school splits and falls on him, right after he wishes he were a child again. The God of the Past grants the wish his own way: Kael wakes in the Thebes market in 1333 B.C.E. in a ten-year-old's body, tries to buy bread with a 10,000 yen bill, and is arrested as a foreign spy.
1873. Where does it happen? => On the cherry blossom road in Japan, then in the market of Thebes.

1876. What kind of event is it? => A death
1877. How does the hero react at first? => Panic and disbelief. He stares at his small hands in a bronze mirror, then hunger wins and he tries to pay for bread.
1878. Does the hero refuse the call at first? => No
1879. What makes them accept? => He has no choice. Once Tutankhamun names him a royal playmate, survival means playing the part.
1880. What's the point of no return? => Tutankhamun's decree in the throne room: "My playmate." From then on Kael belongs to the palace.
1881. What does the hero lose right away? => His life, his adult body, his home, his name and his freedom.
1882. What does the hero gain right away? => A second life, his adult mind and his memory of history, and the attention of a lonely boy king.
1883. How shocking should it be? => 9
1884. Should readers see it coming? => No
1885. Describe the inciting incident as a sequence of beats. => 1. The wish under the cherry trees. 2. The cherry tree cracks and falls, petals, the dark. 3. The God of the Past grants the wish "in his own way." 4. Kael wakes in a ten-year-old's body in the Thebes market. 5. He tries to pay with a 10,000 yen bill, and a crowd gathers. 6. Guards seize him as a foreign spy. 7. He is thrown before the throne, and the bill is shown as evidence. 8. Officials call for death, and Amenhotep watches in silence. 9. Tutankhamun declares him a royal playmate, so he can't be executed.
1886. List the major events between the beginning and the climax, in order. => Kael becomes the pharaoh's playmate (Ch 1); An assassin attacks Tut, and Kael sees that this is normal here (Ch 2); Nakhti agrees to teach Kael court survival (Ch 2); Amenhotep sends Kalici to frame Kael, and she can't scream (Ch 3); Kael and Tut make Kalici his handmaid, openly crossing the Vizier (Ch 4); Tut reveals his father was poisoned (Ch 4); Kael's wager with Nakhtmin and the first clue in the storeroom (Ch 5); The memorial feast trap: Kael knocks the cup away and exposes himself (Ch 6); Kalici spies on the Vizier's house and learns the hooded man's scent (Ch 7); The secret council, the overheard slave and Kael's first killing (Ch 8)
1887. The first obstacle => Execution: officials want the foreign spy dead on the spot (Chapter 1).
1888. The second obstacle => Kalici's false accusation, a plot to have him executed for attacking a servant (Chapter 3).
1889. The third obstacle => The memorial feast trap, which proves to Amenhotep who took the token (Chapter 6).
1890. The first ally the hero gains => Tutankhamun, then Nakhti.
1891. The first enemy the hero faces => Amenhotep the Vizier.
1892. The first small victory => Saving Kalici by having the pharaoh name her his handmaid (Chapter 4).
1893. The first real defeat => The memorial feast. Kael "saves" Tut from a poison that was never there and hands the Vizier proof that he took the token (Chapter 6).
1894. A training or preparation sequence, if any => Nakhti's lessons on who holds power in the palace (Chapter 2), and Nakhtmin starting to teach him to defend himself after the wager (Chapter 5).
1895. A journey or travel sequence, if any => None. Volume 1 stays in Thebes and the palace.
1896. A quiet character moment between big events => Kael and Kalici talking until dawn about her father's death (Chapter 3).
1897. A discovery that changes the plan => Tut's confession that his father was poisoned. It turns Kael from surviving to hunting a killer (Chapter 4).
1898. Where does the hero's plan go wrong? => At the memorial feast, when his move to save Tut proves to Amenhotep that he's the thief.
1899. What new information raises the stakes? => The hooded man's scent, fresh at the foot of the king's bed where Kael sleeps. The Vizier is ready to kill.
1900. What is the villain doing during all this? => Watching, testing and setting traps: first the frame through Kalici, then the feast, then turning Kalici into his informant, and finally sending his steward with a blade.
1901. How quickly should tension rise? => 8
1902. Should there be a false victory before things get worse? => Yes
1903. Describe the rising action as a list of chapter-sized steps. => Ch 1: arrival, arrest, made the king's playmate. Ch 2: an assassin, then a mentor. Ch 3: the frame that fails, and Kalici's story. Ch 4: saving Kalici, and the poisoned father. Ch 5: the wager and the first clue. Ch 6: the memorial feast trap. Ch 7: Kalici in the Vizier's house, the scent. Ch 8: the secret council and the slave Kael kills. Ch 9: the midnight blade and Ren.
1904. What happens at the midpoint of Volume 1? => The memorial feast (Chapter 6). Amenhotep serves the old king's last dinner again to catch whoever took the token. Kael knocks the king's cup away to stop a poisoning that never existed, and the Vizier drinks from the same jar to prove it. The court laughs at a clumsy child, but Amenhotep now knows exactly who has been digging.
1905. What kind of midpoint is it? => A false victory
1906. What does the hero learn at the midpoint? => That he's no longer the hunter. The Vizier has been ahead of him the whole time, and his adult mind gave him away.
1907. How does the story change direction? => From an investigation into the old king's death to a fight to stay alive: Kael becomes the target.
1908. Who is affected most? => Kael, and Nakhtmin, whose cover as the Vizier's man is only saved because Kael moved first.
1909. Does the villain make a big move at the midpoint? => Yes
1910. What's the new goal after the midpoint? => Survive the Vizier's next move while finding the hooded man before he strikes.
1911. Which image marks the midpoint (a likely illustration)? => Kael on a cushion at the boy king's feet, mid-stumble, the gold cup tumbling from Tut's hands while the whole banquet hall freezes.
1912. Does the midpoint end a chapter? => No
1913. How does the hero feel after the midpoint? => Exposed, guilty and afraid to eat. He stops trusting any food he hasn't watched being made.
1914. Which earlier clue pays off at the midpoint? => The guard's token from the Chapter 5 storeroom stakeout, which the feast was built to trace.
1915. Which relationship shifts at the midpoint? => Kael and Nakhtmin: anger first, then a real alliance, since Kael protected his cover.
1916. How big is the midpoint shift? => 8
1917. Describe the midpoint scene in detail. => The great hall at night: lotus flowers, harpists playing a forgotten song, roast goose, figs and honey cakes. Every seat matches the night the old king died. Kael wears a perfumed cone that melts down his face, and Tut laughs at him, the last light moment. Course by course Nakhtmin goes quiet, because he stood guard at that dinner. When the king's cup arrives he whispers that it's the same meal. Kael moves first, fakes a stumble and knocks the cup away. Silence. Nakhtmin has to seize him roughly. Amenhotep calls for the same jar and slowly drinks a full cup, eyes on Kael. Nothing happens. "Children are clumsy," he says, and the hall laughs. Then he bends down and murmurs, "Who taught you to fear that cup, little one?"
1918. Describe the climax of Volume 1. => At midnight in Tutankhamun's bedchamber, smoke rolls over the bed and a blade falls. Kael throws his small body between the steel and the king and takes the stab. The guards have been drawn away by a false alarm. A masked stranger, Ren, drops through the ceiling vent, kills the assassin, presses cloth to Kael's wound and carries him through the corridors to the physician. Kalici recognizes the dead assassin as the Vizier's steward, and the truth comes out: the attack on the king was cover, and Kael was always the target.
1919. Who does the hero face in the climax? => The Vizier's steward, the man who bruised Kalici, sent by Amenhotep.
1920. Where does the climax happen? => Tutankhamun's bedchamber, then the palace corridors and the royal physician's chamber.
1921. What kind of climax? => A sacrifice
1922. What's at stake in the climax? => Tutankhamun's life and Kael's own.
1923. What's the hero's plan going in? => Sleep at the foot of the king's bed, sure that no one would risk the king to reach him.
1924. What goes wrong? => The Vizier was willing to risk the king. The guards are lured away, smoke fills the room, and the blade comes anyway.
1925. What's the hero's darkest moment in the climax? => Bleeding on the floor in the smoke, unable to stand, hearing Tut scream his name and knowing his child's body can't protect anyone.
1926. What turns it around? => Ren dropping through the ceiling vent.
1927. What does the hero sacrifice or risk? => His life. He takes the blade meant to kill both of them.
1928. Does an ally arrive to help? => Yes
1929. Who helps, and how? => Ren, a masked stranger, drops through the vent, kills the assassin, stops the bleeding and carries Kael to the physician. Kalici then identifies the dead steward.
1930. What's the most epic image of the climax (a likely illustration)? => Ren in raven-black leather and a golden mask, landing in the smoke with Kael's blood on his hands, while the boy king stares up from the bed.
1931. Should the climax get a full-page or two-page illustration? => Yes
1932. What's the key line of dialogue in the climax? => Ren: "I do not care about the pharaoh. I have been tasked to save you, Kael."
1933. How does the climax end? => Costly victory
1934. What does winning or losing cost? => A deep wound and a scar Kael will carry, and a death that will be pinned on an innocent man (Nakhti, in the epilogue).
1935. How many pages should the climax take? => 30
1936. Does the hero use a new technique or power in the climax? => No
1937. Which earlier setup pays off in the climax? => Chapter 7: Kael's "one careful truth," that he sleeps at the king's feet, and the myrrh-and-burnt-honey scent at the foot of the bed.
1938. How does the villain react to the outcome? => Calm in public, shocked and grieving for the "loyal" steward. In private he chooses a scapegoat: Nakhti.
1939. Describe the climax beat by beat at its peak. => 1. Smoke crawls over the bed, and lamplight catches steel. 2. Kael moves before he thinks, and the blade goes into his side instead of Tut's chest. 3. Tut screams. The doors are empty, because the guards chased a false alarm. 4. The assassin pulls the blade free for a second strike. 5. The ceiling vent bursts, and a figure in black with a golden mask lands between them. 6. One clean cut, and the assassin falls. 7. The stranger kneels and presses cloth to Kael's wound: "The Vizier's men are still inside." 8. He lifts Kael like he weighs nothing and runs, while Tut shouts for the guards. 9. In the physician's chamber, through blurred vision, Kael sees only his eyes and a carved bronze ring. 10. "I do not care about the pharaoh. I have been tasked to save you, Kael." 11. He is gone. Kael falls into the dark.
1940. What happens right after the climax? => The epilogue, "What the Sand Remembers." Kael wakes in the physician's chamber days later, scarred. Amenhotep publicly mourns his steward and announces that the steward was paid by someone inside the palace. That night, in the empty throne room, Kael tells Tutankhamun, Aneb and Nakhti the truth: he is eighteen, he came from the future, and a god cursed him. Kalici, hiding behind a column, steps out and stands with him. They swear the Nile Pact. At dawn, guards arrest Nakhti for planning the attack on the king, using papyrus "in his hand" as proof. He was framed.
1941. What's the final scene of Volume 1? => Nakhti being led away in chains at dawn while Kael stands in the empty throne room, unable to stop it.
1942. What's the final image? => Kael, small, standing in the long shadow of the empty throne as dawn light comes through the doorway, with the Nile Pact water still wet on his hands.
1943. What's the final line? => "They say gods don't make mistakes. Tonight I decided to become the one god of this age that Egypt can't ignore, child's face and all."
1944. How should the ending feel? => Ominous
1945. What's resolved by the end? => Kael survives. The steward is dead. The team is formed, and Kael's secret is shared with the people he trusts most.
1946. What's left unresolved? => Who the hooded man is, who really poisoned the old king, who Ren is and who sent him, why the God of the Past chose Kael, and the two-year clock.
1947. What's the cliffhanger or hook for Volume 2? => Nakhti is imprisoned for a crime he didn't commit, and the team has to free him without exposing themselves. In the last extra scene, Amenhotep holds Kael's 10,000 yen bill up to a lamp and studies the foreign face printed on it.
1948. What kind of hook? => A character in danger
1949. Who appears in the final pages? => Kael, Tutankhamun, Aneb, Nakhti, Kalici and Amenhotep, then Amenhotep alone in the extra scene.
1950. Should there be a short extra scene after the ending (like a movie's post-credits scene)? => Yes
1951. What happens in that extra scene? => Amenhotep alone in his chambers, turning Kael's 10,000 yen bill toward the lamp and comparing the printed face with the senet piece on his board. "A coin no one in Kemet could make… from a land no map has drawn." He sends for the High Priest.
1952. How has the hero changed by the last page? => He started out only trying to survive. Now he leads: he has killed, shared his secret and chosen people to protect, and he's no longer only playing a child.
1953. What does the ending promise about Volume 2? => A war to free Nakhti, the hunt for the hooded man and the old king's poisoner, and the mystery of Ren and the God of the Past.
1954. Should the ending mirror the opening? => Yes
1955. How does the ending mirror the opening? => It opens with a flash-forward of guards dragging a small blond boy toward the throne, and it ends with the same throne room, Kael now standing there by choice while guards drag Nakhti away.
1956. What should readers say out loud when they reach the last page? => "No… not Nakhti."
1957. Describe the last five pages of Volume 1. => 1. In the empty throne room at night, Kael tells Tut, Aneb and Nakhti the truth: eighteen years old, from the future, cursed by a god. 2. Silence. Aneb draws a knife. Tut stops him. 3. A sound behind a lotus column. Kalici steps out, kneels beside Kael and says she heard all of it and is staying. 4. Kael's inner monologue: he trusts her more than he has trusted anyone, and he doesn't say it. They draw Nile water and swear the Pact: "What flows returns." 5. Dawn: guards pour in on the Vizier's orders and arrest Nakhti for planning the attack on the king. Kael stands alone in the throne's shadow, and the final narration line.
1958. List the subplots in Volume 1. => Hunting the old king's poisoner (Kael and Nakhtmin); Kalici as a double agent in the Vizier's house; Kael and Tut's friendship and Tut's growing will; Kael and Aneb's rivalry; Nakhti's teaching and the father-son bond; Wennefer's suspicion about Kael's curse; Ren and the God of the Past
1959. The most important subplot => Hunting the old king's poisoner.
1960. The romance subplot, if any => None in Volume 1. Kael and Kalici's bond is loyalty, not romance.
1961. The friendship subplot => Kael and Tutankhamun: from a pet and his owner to two boys who trust each other with their lives.
1962. The mystery subplot => Who poisoned the old king, and who is the hooded man who smells of myrrh and burnt honey?
1963. A subplot on the villain's side => Amenhotep turning Kalici into his informant and building the feast trap through the hooded man.
1964. A comedy subplot => None. The only light moments are small ones, like the cat, the perfumed cone and Tut's laugh.
1965. A subplot about the world or its politics => The Vizier's faction against the Pharaoh's faction, and Nakhtmin caught between them as the Vizier's man.
1966. Which subplot ties into the climax? => Kalici's double-agent work. Her report that Kael sleeps at the king's feet is what puts both boys under the blade.
1967. Which subplot continues into Volume 2? => The poisoner hunt, Nakhti's imprisonment and the mystery of Ren.
1968. How many subplots should Volume 1 juggle? => 6
1969. Should any chapter focus on a side character? => Yes
1970. Which side character gets the spotlight? => Kalici (Chapter 7 is told from her point of view).
1971. Which subplot resolves in Volume 1? => Kalici's safety: she is moved under royal protection as Kael's handmaid in Chapter 4.
1972. Which subplot is a red herring (a false lead)? => The guard's token from the storeroom, which points everyone at Nakhtmin's own guards when the real trail leads to the hooded priest.
1973. How do the subplots connect to the main theme? => Every subplot asks the theme's question: what does surviving cost you? Kalici survives by lying for the Vizier, then for Kael. Nakhtmin survives by serving the man he wants to bring down. Tut survives by pretending not to see. Kael survives by playing a child and finally by killing. Each subplot shows a different price, and the epilogue shows the price paid by the one who didn't lie: Nakhti.
1974. What's the biggest twist in Volume 1? => The midnight attack on the pharaoh was a cover. Kael was the target all along, and the Vizier changed his plan once Kael won Tut's trust.
1975. When does it happen? => At the climax
1976. Other reveals in Volume 1 => Kael is in a ten-year-old's body (Ch 1); Assassination attempts on the king are routine (Ch 2); Kalici was sent to frame Kael (Ch 3); The old pharaoh was poisoned (Ch 4); The poisoner is still in the palace (Ch 5); The memorial feast was bait, and there was never poison (Ch 6); The hooded man's scent is in the king's bedchamber (Ch 7); Ren knows the name of the God of the Past (Ch 9); Nakhti is framed for the attack (Epilogue)
1977. Which clue sets up the big twist? => Amenhotep in Chapter 6: "It would be a shame if his fears came true."
1978. Where is that clue placed? => The last line of Chapter 6, and Kalici's report in Chapter 7 that Kael now sleeps at the king's feet.
1979. What red herring misleads readers? => The guard's token from the storeroom, which points at Nakhtmin's own guards.
1980. What's foreshadowed for later volumes? => Ren's master and his link to the God of the Past, the hooded man's identity, the two-year clock, and Tutankhamun's real death in 1323 B.C.E.
1981. Small details to plant in Volume 1, each with what it pays off later => The 10,000 yen bill kept as evidence: Amenhotep studies it in the epilogue, and the priests investigate Kael in Volume 2; The cheap senet piece on Amenhotep's board: Amenhotep's game with Kael; Myrrh and burnt honey: the hooded man's identity in Volume 2; The ceiling vent in Tut's bedchamber: Ren's entrance in Chapter 9; Ren's carved bronze ring: it identifies Ren's order later; Nakhtmin's dust sketches: the seating chart of the night the old king died; Kalici's father the scribe, who wrote down everything: his hidden records; The hourglass from the God of the Past: the two-year countdown
1982. Should a character turn out not to be who they seem? => Yes
1983. Who, and what's their secret? => Kalici. The servant girl sent to frame Kael becomes his double agent inside the Vizier's house.
1984. Should a character die unexpectedly? => No
1985. Who dies? => No main character dies in Volume 1. The deaths are the assassins, the steward and the slave Kael kills in Chapter 8.
1986. Should there be a twist about the hero? => Yes
1987. What's the twist about the hero? => Kael tells his inner circle he is eighteen and from the future. The reader knows, but the characters don't until the epilogue.
1988. Should there be a twist about the world? => Yes
1989. What's the twist about the world? => Someone else in Kemet already knows about the God of the Past: Ren says Kael is not the first outsider he has saved.
1990. How shocking should twists be? => 8
1991. Describe the scene where the biggest twist is revealed. => The physician's chamber, after midnight. Kael is barely conscious, with a cloth pressed into his side. Kalici stares at the dead man the guards have dragged in, and her hand goes to her sleeve where the old bruises were. "That's him. That's the man who hurt me." The Vizier's steward. She shakes as she says the rest: the order was to kill Kael on his mat at the foot of the bed, and the attack on the king was only the cover. Kael lies very still. Everything since the feast was aimed at him, and he put the king in the way by sleeping there.

2005. What pacing problem from other manga or light novels should we avoid? => Filler conversations that repeat what the reader already knows, and explanations dumped in the middle of a tense scene. Every scene has to turn something, the way Steins;Gate never wastes an episode once it gets going.
2006. Should Chapter 1 be a longer "debut" chapter? => Yes, extra long
2007. Chapter 1 title => The Wish Beneath the Cherry Blossoms
2008. The purpose of Chapter 1 in one sentence => To introduce the Main Character (Kael) and setup the plot
2009. What happens in Chapter 1? A summary is fine. => Chapter 1: The Wish Beneath the Cherry Blossoms
Scene 1: The Last Ordinary Morning

Setting: A street in Japan, spring, cherry blossoms falling.
Purpose: Establish who Kael is and what he’s tired of.

Kael walks to school alone under the cherry trees, weighed down by exams, pressure, and a future that feels decided for him.
A few small details show his stress: a phone full of unread messages, a test he dreads, a quiet sigh.
He looks at kids playing nearby and murmurs, “I wish I was a kid again. Things were so much simpler.”
A crack like a gunshot: the oldest cherry tree splits at the roots and falls. Blossoms explode across the pavement, the trunk comes down on him, and then nothing.

Turn: His last wish hangs in the air as everything goes dark.

Scene 2: The God of the Past

Setting: A timeless void, maybe an endless desert of fading memories or a hall of ancient sundials.
Purpose: Explain the isekai rules and plant the series’ bigger mystery.

Kael wakes in a strange place and meets the God of the Past, an ancient and unreadable figure.
The god tells him he died, and that he heard his final wish.
He grants it, but in his own way. Kael will be a child again, in a time long past.
Kael tries to ask why and where, but the god only smiles: “Simple? We shall see.”

Turn: The void dissolves into blinding sunlight.

Scene 3: The Market at Thebes

Setting: A crowded street market in Egypt’s capital.
Purpose: Show the culture shock and the new body.

Kael stumbles awake amid spices, donkeys, linen, and shouting merchants.
He catches his reflection in a bronze mirror or a water jar. He’s about ten years old again.
Panic turns into hunger. He tries to buy bread with the only money he has, a 10,000 yen bill.
The merchant stares at the strange paper with a foreign face printed on it, and a crowd gathers.
Guards push through, call him a foreigner and possibly a spy, and seize him.

Turn: Kael is dragged toward the palace, still clutching the bill.

Scene 4: Before the Throne

Setting: The throne room, grand and tense.
Purpose: Introduce the court and the power gap at the top.

Kael is thrown before the throne, and the bill is presented as evidence.
Officials argue over his fate, with execution as the easy answer. The Vizier Amenhotep watches in silence. This is a quiet first appearance that plants him for Chapter 3.
Nakhtmin stands beside the throne like a wall, his first glimpse before Chapter 2.
Kael looks up and sees that the pharaoh is a 9-year-old boy, Tutankhamun, newly crowned and clearly not in control of his own court.

Turn: The two boys lock eyes, and something in Tut softens.

Scene 5: The Pharaoh’s Decree

Setting: Same throne room.
Purpose: Save Kael and set up the series’ central relationship.

Tut knows he can’t simply overrule his advisors, so he finds a clever way around them.
He declares that the foreign boy will be his playmate. A plaything for the pharaoh can’t be executed.
The officials grumble, and Amenhotep’s eyes narrow.
Kael is led away alive, and realizes his “simple” childhood now depends on the whims of a boy king.

Chapter-ending line: Kael, thinking: “I wished for a simpler life… and the gods gave me a palace full of people who want me dead.”

2030. Will Volume 1 have a Chapter 2? => Yes
2031. Chapter 2 title => The Shield of the Pharaoh
2032. The purpose of Chapter 2 in one sentence => To demonstrate the face that assassination is a common occurrence around the royal court & especially the Pharaoh.
2033. What happens in Chapter 2? A summary is fine. => Chapter 2: The Shield of the Pharaoh
Scene 1: Games in the Royal Chambers

Setting: Tutankhamun’s bedchamber, afternoon.
Purpose: Build the boys’ friendship before danger strikes.

Kael and Tut play together, maybe senet or a game Kael teaches him from modern Japan.
Tut laughs freely for once. Away from the court, he’s just a kid.
Kael starts to relax. Maybe this second childhood won’t be so bad.

Turn: A shadow moves behind the curtain.

Scene 2: The Assassin

Setting: Same chamber.
Purpose: Show the danger and Tut’s grim calm.

An assassin bursts in with a blade aimed at the pharaoh.
Kael freezes in terror, but Tut doesn’t even flinch. He keeps holding his game piece.
Nakhtmin, Commander of the Royal Retainers and Shield of the Living Pharaoh, appears in an instant and cuts the attacker down.
Tut sighs and says something chillingly casual, like “That’s the third this season.”

Turn: Kael realizes this is normal here. Assassination attempts are just part of the routine.

Scene 3: The Weight of It

Setting: Palace corridors, after the attack.
Purpose: Kael’s internal shift from surviving to strategizing.

Kael leaves the chamber, shaking, and his thoughts race.
If the pharaoh lives like this, the pharaoh’s foreign playmate is an even easier target.
He knows nothing about this court: who rules, who plots, who can be trusted.
He decides he needs to learn Egyptian politics, and fast. He needs a teacher.

Turn: He sets out to find someone who knows how the palace really works.

Scene 4: Finding Nakhti

Setting: A quiet place in the palace, such as a scribe’s archive, a garden, or a temple courtyard.
Purpose: Introduce the mentor.

Kael’s search leads him to Nakhti. A natural fit is an aging royal scribe or retired official who has outlived several reigns.
At first Nakhti dismisses him: why should he teach a foreign child?
Kael answers with a sharp question or observation about the assassination that most adults would miss.
Nakhti studies him, intrigued, and agrees to teach him.

Turn: Nakhti agrees, warning that what he teaches could save Kael’s life or get him killed.

Scene 5: The First Lesson

Setting: Same place, evening light.
Purpose: Map the court for Kael and the reader.

Nakhti lays out the most powerful people in the palace: who holds land, who controls temples, and who commands soldiers.
He explains the two factions:
The Vizier’s faction, led by Amenhotep, holds the real power while the pharaoh is young.
The Pharaoh’s faction is loyal to Tut but weaker, protected mainly by Nakhtmin’s guards.
He names those Kael should beware of and the few he can trust.
Kael listens, asks smart questions, and connects threads faster than Nakhti expects.

Chapter-ending line: Nakhti, quietly impressed: “You have the body of a child, but you listen like a man who has already lost something. Wisdom like that is rare, and in this palace, it’s dangerous.”

2055. Chapter 3 title => Kalici’s I’ll fated ruse
2056. The purpose of Chapter 3 in one sentence => introduce Amenhotep as a cruel, patient enemy who uses a hurt child to destroy Kael, and show Kael turning that weapon into an ally through kindness, earning Kalici’s lifelong loyalty.
2057. What happens in Chapter 3? A summary is fine. => Scene 1: The Vizier

Setting: Amenhotep’s private chambers, night, lit by oil lamps.
Purpose: Introduce the main antagonist in full.

Open on Amenhotep alone, calm and polished, the true power behind the young throne.
He sees the foreign playmate as a threat. Tut listens to Kael, and a pharaoh with a friend is a pharaoh who might grow a spine.
He summons Kalici, a child servant with nowhere else to go.
He gives her an order: go to Kael’s chamber tonight, then run out screaming that the foreign boy attacked her in a rage. A foreigner who strikes a palace servant in the pharaoh’s own household would be executed.
When she hesitates, he adds that his steward will make sure her bruises are convincing. The line is quiet and almost gentle, which makes it chilling.
If she obeys, she can live in the palace under his protection for life. If she refuses, she’ll be thrown into the streets or worse.

Turn: Kalici agrees. She is more afraid of the man in front of her than of anything she’ll have to do.

Scene 2: The Intruder

Setting: Kael’s bedchamber, late night.
Purpose: The confrontation.

Kael lies awake, replaying Nakhti’s lessons about whom to trust.
The door creaks, and Kalici slips in, looking hollow and miserable. Her arm is wrapped in her sleeve to hide the fresh marks.
She stands by the door and draws a breath to scream, but can’t make the sound.
Kael sits up and sees the bruises and her shaking. He quietly asks who hurt her.
She blurts out that she’s supposed to say he did it. He doesn’t call the guards or get angry. He tells her she doesn’t have to do this, and asks what’s made her so sad.

Turn: No one in the palace has ever asked her that, and she breaks down.

Scene 3: Kalici’s Story

Setting: Same chamber, through the night.
Purpose: Reveal her backstory and the emotional heart of the chapter.

Kalici tells Kael about her father’s death and how it left her alone and at the Vizier’s mercy.
Optional series hook: her father served near the old pharaoh’s table and died around the same time, which quietly connects to Tut’s poison secret in Chapter 4.
Kael listens and comforts her. Remembering his own death and loneliness, he tells her that losing someone doesn’t make her worthless, and that what was done to her arm was never her fault.
They talk until dawn.

Turn: For the first time, Kalici feels safe with someone.

Scene 4: Dawn

Setting: The chamber at sunrise.
Purpose: Seal her loyalty and set up Chapter 4.

Kalici slips out before the palace wakes. She never screamed.
At the door, she looks back at Kael with fierce, quiet devotion. She’ll protect the boy who protected her.
Alone, Kael realizes the Vizier has made his first move, and that it came through a hurt child.

Chapter-ending line: Kael, thinking: “He didn’t send a soldier to kill me. He sent a frightened little girl and hurt her first. That’s the kind of enemy Amenhotep is.”

2078. Will Volume 1 have a Chapter 4? => Yes
2079. Chapter 4 title => The protagonist saves Kalici from Amenhotep's wrath
2080. The purpose of Chapter 4 in one sentence => The protagonist discovers a hidden faction within the palace plotting against Tutankhamen, forcing him to choose between exposing them or leveraging their secrets for survival.
2081. What happens in Chapter 4? A summary is fine. => Scene 1: The Report

Setting: Amenhotep’s private chambers, early morning.
Purpose: Show how dangerous the Vizier is and make Kalici a double agent.

Amenhotep summons Kalici to hear how the frame job went.
Kalici lies. She says she lost her nerve, was too frightened to go through with it, and fled Kael’s room in fear.
She leaves out the truth: Kael was kind to her, and they talked through the night about her father’s death.
She has secretly chosen Kael’s side and decided to spy on the Vizier for him.
She politely asks Amenhotep for a different task so she can stay in the palace.
Amenhotep smiles and agrees, but says he’ll assign it “later.”

Turn: Kalici reads the lie in his calm voice. “Later” means he plans to have her killed or thrown out of the palace.

Scene 2: The Escape

Setting: Palace corridors, then Kael’s chamber.
Purpose: Raise the stakes and bring the two leads together.

Kalici hurries through the halls, sure she’s being watched.
She reaches Kael and tells him everything: the Vizier’s plot, her lie, and the danger she’s in now.
Kael doesn’t hesitate. He agrees to help her.
They realize only the pharaoh can protect her, and they have to move before the Vizier does.

Turn: Kael decides to spend his only real asset, his friendship with Tutankhamun.

Scene 3: The Audience

Setting: The pharaoh’s chambers or a small audience hall.
Purpose: Kael’s first political move, and Tut using his authority.

Kael and Kalici rush to an audience with Tutankhamun.
Kael asks that Kalici be made his personal handmaid, which would put her under royal protection.
Tut agrees, partly out of friendship and partly because he enjoys overruling the Vizier.
The pharaoh makes it an official announcement in front of witnesses.
If you want, a brief beat can show Amenhotep hearing the news and looking calm on the surface but furious underneath.

Turn: Kalici’s life is saved. But Kael has now openly crossed the Vizier.

Scene 4: One Week Later

Setting: The throne room, quiet, after the court has left.
Purpose: Deepen Tut and plant a major mystery for the series.

Things have settled. Kalici serves Kael, and the palace seems calm.
Tutankhamun, alone with Kael, confides a secret: his father didn’t die naturally. He was poisoned.
Kael is shocked. The ruler of Egypt couldn’t protect his own father.
He realizes again that power doesn’t mean safety in this court.

Chapter-ending line: Kael’s inner thought: “If even the pharaoh can’t stay safe… how safe am I?”

2102. Will Volume 1 have a Chapter 5? => Yes
2103. Chapter 5 title => The Royal Guard Captain’s Wager
2104. The purpose of Chapter 5 in one sentence => Bring Nakhtmin forward as a character and launch the mystery.
2105. What happens in Chapter 5? A summary is fine. => Chapter 5: The Royal Guard Captain’s Wager
Scene 1: The Question

Setting: Palace training yard, dawn.
Purpose: Bring Nakhtmin forward as a character and launch the mystery.

Kael can’t stop thinking about Tut’s secret, so he seeks out Nakhtmin while the guards drill.
He asks Nakhtmin directly what really happened to the old pharaoh.
Nakhtmin goes cold. He was a young guard back then, and the death happened on his watch, or his commander’s.
Kael reveals that Tut told him it was poison.

Turn: Nakhtmin realizes the boy king trusts this foreign child with secrets he has never told his own guards.

Scene 2: The Wager

Setting: Same yard, away from the other guards.
Purpose: Set the stakes and form an unlikely partnership.

Nakhtmin insists the poisoner is long gone. Years have passed, the court moved, and the old servants were scattered or died.
Kael disagrees. Whoever did it had access and patience, and people like that don’t leave power behind. He bets the killer is still in the palace.
They shake on it. If Kael is wrong, he stays out of palace security for good. If Kael is right, Nakhtmin helps him hunt the killer and teaches him to defend himself.
Nakhtmin agrees mostly to humor him, though part of him has always wondered.

Turn: Kael has given himself a deadline, maybe one month, to prove the killer is still close.

Scene 3: Gathering Allies

Setting: Kael’s chamber, evening.
Purpose: Put the cast to work and use the double-agent setup from Chapter 4.

Kael brings Nakhti in. Nakhti warns him that digging into the old pharaoh’s death is the fastest way to get killed in this court.
Nakhti still helps. He lists who served close to the old pharaoh’s table and who is still in the palace today.
Kalici offers to listen in the Vizier’s household, since servants talk and nobody notices a servant girl.
Kael is torn about putting her at risk, but she insists this is her choice.

Turn: One name on Nakhti’s list is someone close to Tut right now. Kael keeps that to himself for the moment.

Scene 4: The First Clue

Setting: The palace kitchens or storerooms.
Purpose: The investigation’s first real lead.

Kael questions an old kitchen servant who remembers the night the pharaoh fell ill.
She recalls a detail no one asked about back then. Someone unfamiliar handled the wine that night, or a cup was carried away before anyone could inspect it.
Kael uses what he knows from modern detective stories: timelines, who had access, who benefited.
Kalici returns with servant gossip that someone in the Vizier’s circle still visits the old storerooms at night.

Turn: The two clues point to the same place.

Scene 5: The Captain’s Doubt Breaks

Setting: The storeroom, night.
Purpose: Kael wins the bet, and the chapter climaxes.

Kael and Nakhtmin stake out the storeroom together.
A hooded figure comes and goes, removing or hiding something. They don’t catch them, but Nakhtmin recognizes the walk, or the guard’s token they drop.
Nakhtmin goes pale. It’s someone he knows and who still serves in the palace.
He admits Kael won the bet, and the alliance becomes real.

Chapter-ending line: Nakhtmin, quietly: “You were right, boy. The snake never left the garden. It’s been living in it.”

2126. Will Volume 1 have a Chapter 6? => Yes
2127. Chapter 6 title => The Dead King’s Last Dinner, Served Again
2128. The purpose of Chapter 6 in one sentence => Let the Vizier’s trap outplay Kael, exposing him as the one who took the token and turning him from the hunter into the hunted.
2129. What happens in Chapter 6? A summary is fine. => Chapter 6: The Dead King’s Last Dinner, Served Again

Scene 1: The Missing Token (interlude)
Setting: Amenhotep’s private chambers, the night after the stakeout.
Purpose: Show the trap being built, so readers know more than Kael does.

The hooded figure reports the token is gone. Someone was in the old storeroom.
Amenhotep wants a search. The figure says that would only warn the thief.
The plan: serve the old king’s last dinner again, with the same guests, seats, dishes and song. Officially he died of sickness, so only someone who knows the truth would see danger in it. “Whoever stops the king from eating took the token.”
Amenhotep will call it a memorial feast for Tut’s father, a normal custom no one will question.

Turn: Readers know the feast is a trap. Kael doesn’t.

Scene 2: A Feast for the Old King
Setting: Tut’s chambers, then Kael’s chamber, the next day.
Purpose: Make the trap personal for Tut and Kael.

Tut is overjoyed. Since the court left his father’s city, almost no one speaks of the old king.
Nakhti is suspicious: “The Vizier never gives a gift without a hook in it.”
Kalici hears the Vizier has called back old cooks from the old city, and musicians are rehearsing a forgotten song.
Kael and Nakhtmin agree to watch every cup. If anyone makes a move, they’ll be ready.

Turn: Kael walks toward the trap believing he’s the one keeping watch.

Scene 3: The Banquet Hall
Setting: The great hall at night: lotus flowers, harpists, roast goose, figs and honey cakes.
Purpose: Build dread course by course, with one light moment first.

Kael has to wear a perfumed cone on his head like the other guests. It melts down his face, and Tut laughs at him.
Tut sits in his father’s seat, too big for him. Every seat matches that night, so Kael sits on a cushion at Tut’s feet. Kalici serves behind him. Nakhtmin guards the king.
Course by course, Nakhtmin goes quiet. He stood guard at that dinner, and he knows this song, these dishes, and these seats.
Kael sees Nakhtmin’s face change but can’t ask why in front of the court.

Turn: The final course arrives, with the king’s cup of wine.

Scene 4: The Last Cup
Setting: Same hall.
Purpose: Kael springs the trap, and the chapter climaxes.

Nakhtmin whispers that this is the same dinner, down to the seats and the song. That cup may be poisoned.
Nakhtmin starts forward, but Kael moves first. Remembering the councilor poisoned at the Chapter 2 feast, he fakes a stumble and knocks the cup from Tut’s hands.
The hall goes silent. Nakhtmin has to play his part and seize Kael roughly.
Amenhotep calls for the same jar and slowly drinks a cup himself, eyes on Kael. Nothing happens. “Children are clumsy,” he tells the court. Everyone laughs.
Tut is hurt that his father’s feast was spoiled, but he still orders everyone to leave Kael alone.

Turn: Amenhotep murmurs to Kael, “Who taught you to fear that cup, little one?” There was never any poison. The feast was bait, and Kael took it.

Scene 5: Two Conversations in the Dark
Setting: Kael’s chamber late that night, then Amenhotep’s chambers.
Purpose: Show the cost, give Kael one clue, and set up the assassination attempt.

Nakhtmin is furious, then admits the trap was built for someone like him, who stood guard that night. By moving first, Kael kept Nakhtmin’s cover as the Vizier’s man.
Kael regrets it anyway. The enemy now knows who took the token.
Nakhtmin sketches the hall in the dust, his old habit. The feast just showed them everyone who was in that room the night the old king died. The poisoner is probably on that list.
Interlude: the figure tells Amenhotep it’s the boy, and that Nakhtmin leaned toward him first. Amenhotep waves that off. The king’s playmate can’t be killed openly, so it will be done quietly.
Amenhotep picks up a cheap senet piece from the floor, like the one he ignored in Chapter 3, and studies it.

Chapter-ending line: Amenhotep: “The boy is so afraid of poison. It would be a shame if his fears came true.”

2149. Notes, extra ideas or must-have moments for Chapter 6 => Must-have moments and choices:
- The cup is never poisoned. Amenhotep drinks from the same jar himself, so Kael’s move gains nothing and gives away his secret.
- Publicly it’s a memorial feast for Tut’s father, so no one questions why every detail matches that night.
- Kael sits on a cushion at Tut’s feet as the king’s playmate, because every real seat is assigned to match that night.
- Kael moving first keeps Nakhtmin’s cover as the Vizier’s man. The hooded figure noticed Nakhtmin lean toward Kael, which can come back later.
- Callbacks: the councilor poisoned at the Chapter 2 feast, and the cheap senet piece from Chapter 3.
- Keep the hooded figure’s face hidden, but give readers one detail to recognize later, like a voice, a ring or a smell.
- The ending line foreshadows the poison in Kael’s later assassination attempt, where Ren saves him.
2150. Will Volume 1 have a Chapter 7? => Yes
2151. Chapter 7 title => Nobody Notices a Servant Girl
2152. The purpose of Chapter 7 in one sentence => Through Kalici’s eyes, catch the hooded figure’s one recognizable detail, then let the Vizier pull her back in, so Kael has to choose between pulling her out and keeping her in place.
2153. What happens in Chapter 7? A summary is fine. => Chapter 7: Nobody Notices a Servant Girl

Scene 1: The Boy Who Watched a Cat
Setting: Kael’s chamber, morning, three days after the memorial feast.
Purpose: Switch to Kalici’s point of view, show what the feast cost Kael, and send her into the Vizier’s household.

Kalici narrates this whole chapter.
Kael hasn’t eaten properly since the feast. He’s sure the Vizier will try poison next.
An hour ago a palace cat stole the fish off his plate. Kael has been lying on his stomach ever since, watching it to see if it dies. It falls asleep in the sun, fat and happy, and Kalici laughs for the first time in days.
She brings him bread she watched being baked, carried herself and bit first. Kael is angry she took the risk.
They need the hooded figure’s name, and he reports to the Vizier at night. The old servants in the Vizier’s household still let Kalici help with the lamps. Nobody notices a servant girl.
Kael gives her three rules: don’t look at his face, don’t follow him, come straight back.

Turn: Kael lets her go, and Kalici can see he hates himself for it.

Scene 2: Lamp Oil and Myrrh
Setting: A narrow servants’ corridor outside Amenhotep’s private chambers, over three nights.
Purpose: Kalici catches the hooded figure’s one recognizable detail, and gets noticed.

For two nights, nothing. On the third, the Vizier sends his own guards away early.
The hooded figure arrives. Kalici kneels with her eyes on the floor, the way servants do.
Through the door she hears pieces. The boy eats nothing he hasn’t watched being made. The captain leaned toward the boy at the feast. Then the Vizier: “Patience. A frightened boy gets tired. A tired boy gets careless.”
The corridor is so narrow that the figure’s cloak brushes her shoulder as he leaves. She never sees his face, but she breathes in his scent: myrrh and burnt honey, too costly for a servant.
He stops, lifts her lamp and asks whose servant she is. Every part of her wants to run, the way she always does. She stays kneeling and tells the truth, because a lie would be worse: she’s the playmate’s handmaid, and the old servants let her help with the lamps.
He sets the lamp down and walks away without a word.

Turn: Kalici has the detail Kael needed. But the hooded man has seen her face, and she still hasn’t seen his.

Scene 3: A Smell Is Not Proof
Setting: Kael’s chamber, late that night.
Purpose: Turn the scent into a lead, and let Kalici read what Nakhtmin won’t say.

Kalici runs back and tells Kael everything in a rush.
Kael understands the plan. The Vizier is waiting for him to slip, and the poison will come when he relaxes.
Nakhtmin arrives, and Kalici repeats what she overheard. The Vizier knows Nakhtmin leaned toward Kael at the feast.
When Kalici says “myrrh and burnt honey,” Nakhtmin’s hand stops drawing in the dust.
Kael asks if he knows the scent. Nakhtmin: “Half the court burns myrrh. A smell is not proof.” With the Vizier already watching him, a wrong name now gets them all killed.

Turn: Kalici reads faces better than anyone in the palace. She’s sure the captain knows exactly who wears that scent, and that he’s afraid of it.

Scene 4: The Task, Later
Setting: Amenhotep’s private chambers, the next evening.
Purpose: Amenhotep keeps his Chapter 4 promise and pulls Kalici back in.

The summons comes through the same steward who bruised her arm in Chapter 3.
The room smells of myrrh and burnt honey. The hooded man has already told the Vizier about her.
The cheap senet piece Amenhotep once tipped onto the floor is back on his board. He moves it one square while he talks.
He’s gentle, as always. Frightened girls run to whoever is closest, he says, and he forgives her for running to the pharaoh.
He promised her a different task, and here it is: tell him what the playmate eats, who carries his water and where he sleeps.
Then, almost kindly: “Your father was a scribe. He wrote down everything he saw. Don’t be like your father.”
Kalici keeps her hands still so she won’t play with her braids.

Turn: She understands what she’s being asked for: the way to poison Kael quietly. She bows and says, “Yes, my lord.”

Scene 5: Right Where You Sleep
Setting: Kael’s chamber, then the pharaoh’s bedchamber, night.
Purpose: Kael decides whether to pull Kalici out, and the chapter ends on the scent.

Kael wants to pull her out at once. She’ll never set foot in the Vizier’s house again.
Nakhti stops him. A spy who suddenly stops visiting the house she spied on has confessed. And the Vizier will learn something from whatever she says, even a lie.
Kalici sees the calm on Kael’s face and asks, “What mask are you hiding today?” He admits it: keeping her there makes him no better than the man who used her first.
Kalici: “The Vizier gave me orders. You asked me.” She chooses to go back.
Kael’s rule: tell the Vizier only the truth, so she can never be caught lying, and save the lie for when it counts. Then make the truth a shield. From tonight he eats only from the king’s own dishes and sleeps on a mat at the foot of the king’s bed. No one would risk the king to reach a playmate.
Tut is thrilled to have company at night.
Kalici gives the Vizier her report. He smiles: “A boy who never leaves the king’s side is very easy to find.”
Late that night, Kalici carries water to the king’s bedchamber. Tut is asleep. Kael lies awake on his mat.
At the door she stops. The room smells of myrrh and burnt honey, fresh, and strongest at the foot of the bed where Kael’s mat lies.
Kael goes very still, the way he does when he’s hiding a thought. For once, Kalici can’t read the mask.

Chapter-ending line: Kalici, whispering: “Kael… he’s been in this room tonight. Right where you sleep.”

Purpose: Kalici catches the hooded figure’s one recognizable detail, and gets noticed.; Scene 3: A Smell Is Not Proof  Setting: Kael’s chamber, late that night. Purpose: Kael decides whether to pull Kalici out, and the chapter ends on the scent.

2171. How does Chapter 7 end? => Kael keeps Kalici in place and has her give the Vizier one careful truth: Kael now eats only from the king’s dishes and sleeps at the foot of the king’s bed, sure no one will risk the king to reach him. That night Kalici smells myrrh and burnt honey in the king’s bedchamber. The hooded man has already been there, which sets up the attack on Tut and Kael.
2172. What kind of ending does Chapter 7 have? => Cliffhanger
2173. Notes, extra ideas or must-have moments for Chapter 7 => Must-have moments and choices:
- The whole chapter is Kalici’s point of view. Write it in first person like the rest of the book, in her voice: soft, quick to ramble and always reading faces. Kael’s narration returns in Chapter 8.
- The hooded figure’s one detail is his scent: myrrh and burnt honey. It’s costly, which hints he’s more than he seems. Use the same words every time he appears, and never show his face. A smell also avoids mix-ups with Amenhotep’s rings and Ren’s copper ring.
- Kalici never sees his face, but he sees hers. The danger now runs one way.
- In the corridor Kalici wants to run, the way she always does, and stays. It’s a small step in her growth.
- Callbacks: “nobody notices a servant girl” from Chapter 5, the “different task” Amenhotep promised her “later” in Chapter 4, the steward from Chapter 3, and the cheap senet piece from Chapters 3 and 6.
- Nakhtmin goes still at the scent but won’t name anyone. That keeps alive the Chapter 5 hint that he recognized the figure, and Kalici is sure he knows.
- Kael’s rule for a spy: tell the enemy only the truth, so she can’t be caught lying, and save the lie for when it counts. His truth ties his life to Tut’s, which sets up the attempt on both of them in the king’s bedchamber, where Ren saves Kael.
- When Kael goes still at the end, readers who remember Chapter 5 know he’s thinking of the one name on Nakhti’s list that he kept to himself.
- Optional hook: Amenhotep’s line about Kalici’s father hints he was executed for something he saw, which could tie him to the old king’s poisoning. Drop the line if you don’t want that link.
- History note: cats were well loved and linked to the goddess Bastet, so Kael would never test food on one on purpose. The cat steals the fish on its own.
2174. Will Volume 1 have a Chapter 8? => Yes
2175. Chapter 8 title => The Slave Who Heard Too Much, and the Blood That Will Not Wash Off
2176. The purpose of Chapter 8 in one sentence => Form the secret alliance against the Vizier, and make Kael cross a line he can never uncross by killing to protect it.
2177. What happens in Chapter 8? A summary is fine. => Kael's narration returns. The morning after Kalici smells myrrh at the foot of the king's bed, Kael and Nakhtmin search the bedchamber and find a loose grate over the ceiling vent, the way the hooded man came in. Kael keeps sleeping there anyway, because moving would warn the Vizier. Wennefer, the High Priest of Amun, arrives to "bless" the king's foreign playmate and corners Kael about knowledge no child should have. Kael gives him a partial truth. That night in Tut's chambers, Kael, Tut, Aneb and Nakhtmin hold their first secret council: Nakhtmin admits he will spy on the Vizier from inside, and they agree to join forces against him. Aneb argues with Kael the whole time. A young water-carrier slave has heard everything from behind the curtain and runs toward the Vizier's wing. Kael chases him into a dark corridor and kills him with his dagger before he can reach the door. Aneb finds him kneeling over the body. They hide it together without a word. At dawn, word spreads that Wennefer was taken to the Vizier's house for questioning and that a masked stranger broke him out. Kael scrubs his hands until they bleed. That night he lies down at the foot of the king's bed again and hears the vent grate creak.

Kael, Tut, Aneb and Nakhtmin agree to join forces against the Vizier, and Aneb clashes with Kael.; Scene 4: The Curtain. A slave overhears, runs, and Kael kills him in the dark corridor. Aneb finds him.; Scene 5: What Can't Be Washed. Wennefer is taken and freed by a masked stranger, Kael scrubs his hands, and the vent creaks.

2197. Notes, extra ideas or must-have moments for Chapter 8 => This is Kael's lowest point in Volume 1, the scene the story uses for "he's forced to kill an innocent person to protect his secret." Don't soften it: the slave is innocent, only frightened. Keep it quiet and fast, with no music and no speech. Kael's narration goes cold and clipped and then stops entirely for one blank line. Aneb seeing it starts the shift from rival to unlikely ally: from here on they share a secret neither can tell. Wennefer's torture happens off the page, and only the rumor reaches Kael (Ren's first unseen rescue).

2200. The purpose of Chapter 9 in one sentence => Do introduce Ren & depict the assassination attempt on Kael & the pharaoh.
2201. What happens in Chapter 9? A summary is fine. => The assassin's blade slips into the royal bed chamber at midnight. Kael throws himself between it and Tutankhamen and takes the stab wound. Smoke fills the room, and the guards are drawn away by a false alarm. A masked stranger drops through the ceiling vent, presses cloth to Kael's wound, and whispers that the Vizier's men are still inside. He lifts Kael and carries him through the palace corridors to the physician while Tut shouts for the guards. Kael sees only the stranger's eyes and one carved bronze ring.
The masked stranger Ren performs first aid on Kael in the physician's chamber while speaking with a calm, almost apologetic tone. He knows the name of the god of the past and hints that Kael is not the first outsider he has rescued. Before the guards arrive, Ren vanishes through a side passage, leaving Kael unable to tell whether he is an enemy, a servant of the god, or something else entirely.
Kalici recognizes the dead assassin as Amenhotep's steward, the man who threatened her into framing Kael. She confesses that the Vizier's plan was to kill Kael in his cot, and that the attack on the pharaoh was a cover. Kael realizes the real target was always him, and that Amenhotep changed the plan after Kael won Tutankhamen's trust.

2328. Describe the most satisfying reveal in Volume 1. => Kalici identifying the dead steward in the physician's chamber (Chapter 9). The reader realizes the Vizier has been aiming at Kael since the night he first sent a frightened girl to his door.

Describe images you can already picture in your head. => Kael walking alone down cherry blossom road at dawn, school bag on his shoulder, petals drifting past, the last ordinary morning before the crossing.; Aneb's bronze spear pressed under Kael's chin in the training yard while soldiers ring them, Kael's small fists still raised, refusing to step back.; Amenhotep's ringed hand resting on the senet board beside Kael's trembling fingers, one oil lamp between them, the Vizier smiling as he waits for Kael's move.; Tutankhamun and Kael sharing dates on the palace roof under stars, the Nile silver below, the child king laughing as Kael tells him a story from another world.
2330. A moment inside the volume that's worthy of the cover => Kael on a cushion at the boy king's feet in the memorial-feast banquet hall, eyes cold, while the whole court laughs at him.
2331. An image fans would want as a poster => Kael and Tutankhamun back to back in the throne room, the small blond foreigner and the boy in the gold collar, the throne's shadow falling across both.
2332. A silhouette image => Ren crouched in the ceiling vent against the lamplight, a golden mask the only bright thing.
2333. A close-up on someone's eyes => Kael's blue eyes through a haze of myrrh smoke: a ten-year-old's face with an eighteen-year-old's stare.
2334. An image with no words that says everything => Kael alone scrubbing bloodied hands in a basin after killing the slave (Chapter 8).
2335. An image that shows the scale of the world => Kael on the palace roof at dawn, looking over the Nile valley, green river, endless sand, temples and the Great Obelisk of Amun-Ra.
2336. The hero at their lowest => Kael bleeding on the floor after the blade, smoke curling over the bed (Chapter 9).
2337. The hero at their most heroic => Kael throwing himself between the falling blade and the sleeping king (Chapter 9).

2339. The whole cast together => The Nile Pact: Kael, Tut, Aneb, Nakhti and Kalici with wet hands, in the throne room in the epilogue.
2340. Beauty in the middle of chaos => Lotus flowers floating in a banquet bowl while the court laughs and Kael's fists shake under the table (Chapter 6).
2341. An image with rain, blowing sand or falling petals => Cherry petals falling around Kael on the road in Japan, mirrored by desert sand blowing past him in Thebes.
2342. A figure lit from behind => Nakhti in chains in the throne-room doorway at dawn, the sun behind him (epilogue).
2343. An image that mirrors another one later => The opening flash-forward of guards dragging Kael to the throne, mirrored by guards dragging Nakhti away in the epilogue.
2344. Describe the one image you'd want people to remember from Volume 1. => Kael, ten years old in a ragged linen kilt, standing in the empty throne room at dawn. The gold throne behind him is far too big, and its shadow stretches across the floor and swallows him, while a single cherry petal that should not exist in Egypt lies at his feet.
2345. What kind of place is location 1? => A school or academy
2346. Location 1's name => Cherry blossom road 
2347. Describe Cherry blossom road: what do we see when we arrive? => Kael arrives at the cherry blossom road in the early morning, and pale pink petals drift across the wet pavement and the school gate beyond. Students chatter, a vending machine hums, and a phone buzzes in his blazer pocket. The air smells of rain and spring. Nothing looks wrong yet, which makes the calm feel fragile.
2348. The mood of Cherry blossom road => Warm; Cozy; Grand
2349. The architecture or landscape of Cherry blossom road => A quiet residential lane of old cherry trees leads to a grey concrete school gate, with a humming vending machine and a bus shelter, all wet with morning rain.
2350. The main color of Cherry blossom road => #ff8972 [Cherry blossom Pink ]
2351. How is Cherry blossom road lit (time of day, light sources)? => Low morning sun slants through the old cherry branches, lighting drifting petals gold while the grey school gate stays in cold shadow.
2352. The sounds and smells of Cherry blossom road => Wet concrete and sweet spring rain fill the air. A vending machine hums beside the bus shelter, students chatter at the gate, and Kael's blazer phone buzzes with unread exam messages.
2353. Who lives or works at Cherry blossom road? => Old Mrs. Hayashi sweeps the petals every dawn outside her tea shop. She once studied old texts with Kael's historian parents and still keeps their unpaid book order.
2354. What important event happens at Cherry blossom road in Volume 1? => Half way through the walk a cherry blossom tree falls on the hero Kael & he dies (at age 18 not 10). He then faces the god of the past and is then traveled back in time to ancient Egypt.
2355. Which chapters feature Cherry blossom road? => Only Chapter 1 uses Cherry blossom road: Kael walks past Mrs. Hayashi's sweeping, then dies beneath the falling tree as the god's wish takes hold.
2356. A hidden detail or secret about Cherry blossom road => Beneath the oldest cherry tree's roots lies a sealed stone shrine marked with the God of the Past's sigil, which Mrs. Hayashi swept clean each dawn without knowing why.
2357. A landmark or prop at Cherry blossom road that should look the same every time => The oldest cherry tree always drops the same low branch onto the wet pavement at dawn, its trunk split around the roots of a sealed stone shrine.
2358. Does Cherry blossom road get damaged or changed in Volume 1? => Yes
2359. Describe the best wide view of Cherry blossom road, as an opening description or an illustration. => From the top of the slope, the cherry blossom road unrolls like a pink ribbon. Old trees arch over wet pavement while low sun gilds the drifting petals and leaves the grey school gate in cold shadow. Mrs. Hayashi sweeps outside her tea shop, the vending machine hums beside the bus shelter, and Kael walks alone, small against the blossom-lit lane.
2360. What kind of place is location 2? => The main city
2361. Location 2's name => Ancient Egypt Capital Food Market
2362. Describe this place: what do we see when we arrive? => Kael steps out of the shadowed lane into the Thebes food market at midday, where mud-brick stalls sag under linen awnings. The air is thick with smoke from roasting emmer bread, salted fish, onions, and sour beer. Barges unload grain at the Nile wharf while vendors shout prices in a language he barely understands. Flies swarm over dried figs, and a child's cry rises above the bargaining. Every stall owner watches his foreign clothes and small body with suspicion.
2363. The mood of this place => Grand
2364. The architecture or landscape of this place => Mud-brick stalls under sagging linen awnings, a dusty square that slopes down to the Nile wharf, with barges, granaries and the Great Obelisk of Amun-Ra rising above it all.

2367. The sounds and smells of this place => Shouting merchants, donkeys braying, the slap of barges against the wharf. Smoke from emmer bread, salted fish, onions, sour beer and rotten meat in the sun.
2368. Who lives or works at this place? => Bread sellers, fishmongers, beer brewers, barge porters, beggars and the market guards who arrest Kael.

Beyond the river the desert glows white, and over everything rises the Great Obelisk of Amun-Ra, a single needle of stone throwing a long, thin shadow across the crowd.
2375. What kind of place is location 3? => A headquarters or base
2376. Location 3's name => The Ancient Egyptian royal palace throne room
2377. Describe this place: what do we see when we arrive? => Kael is thrown into a vast hall of painted lotus columns. He has his hands tied with rope as a criminal. The room is lit by bronze oil lamps that send myrrh smoke curling toward a gold-leafed ceiling. The throne sits on a tall dais, so high that Tutankhamen's small feet barely reach the footrest. Courtiers in white linen line the walls like a painted audience. Amenhotep stands nearest the dais, closer to the throne than any noble should dare.
2378. The mood of this place => Grand; Dangerous; Sacred
2379. The architecture or landscape of this place => A vast hall of painted lotus columns, a gold-leafed ceiling, and a tall dais with a gold throne far too big for the boy who sits on it.
2380. The main color of this place => #d4a017 [Throne gold]

At the far end, small and alone on a gold throne built for a grown king, a nine-year-old boy in a gold collar looks down at a tied-up foreign child. Kalici enters Kael's room with the intend to frame him for the crime of harming her to damage his reputation in the court.

2450. Describe the narrator's personality, if there is one. => Kael is a cold, precise narrator who keeps a ledger of every betrayal and debt, speaking with the calm of someone who has already decided how to repay everyone. He is proud and sharp, but his composure slips in the gaps between sentences, where his 18-year-old fear shows through. He rarely admits weakness outright, letting the reader catch it instead.
